                                                    iv
How to Use This Guide
This guide follows the order of the course and of the main text, Freund. It is meant to be used three
times: to prepare for Midterm II now, to consolidate Midterm I material, and to review the whole
course for the final. Every unit has the same six parts.

 1. Reading guide. The Freund sections first, then a short list of focused readings from the other
    four books, each with a sentence on what it adds.
 2. Core concepts. Definitions and theorems in Freund’s notation and numbering (cited as, e.g.,
    [Freund Ch. 3, Def. 13]), with the formulas you must know cold collected in a key formulas
    box.
 3. Worked examples. Homework- and exam-style problems solved in full, written the way a
    grader wants to see them.
 4. Pitfalls and exam strategy. The mistakes that cost the most points, and how to organise an
    answer.
 5. Recommended problems. Freund exercises first, then problems from each supplementary
    text.
 6. Mock exam. About ten questions modelled on the homework and on Midterm I, worth 100
    points. Full solutions are in Appendix A; each exam header links to them.

Part IV adds two full-length practice exams in the exact format of Midterm I: a Mock Midterm II
and a cumulative Mock Final. The appendices hold all solutions and a formula sheet with every
named distribution.

The five texts

Tag    Book                                                Best used for
[F]    Miller & Miller, John E. Freund’s Mathematical      The course text: notation, theorem numbering,
       Statistics with Applications, 8th ed. (Pearson,     and problems in the style of the homework.
       2014)                                               Answers to odd-numbered exercises are at the
                                                           end of each chapter.
[BT]   Bertsekas & Tsitsiklis, Introduction to             Intuition and clean derivations, especially
       Probability (MIT lecture notes, 2000)               conditioning, conditional expectation, transforms
                                                           (MGFs), and limit theorems.
[CB]   Casella & Berger, Statistical Inference, 2nd ed.    A rigorous second pass: transformations, MGFs,
                                                           multivariate distributions, sampling from the
                                                           normal, order statistics. Graduate level; selected
                                                           problems only.
[W]    Wasserman, All of Statistics (Springer, 2004)       A compact, precise summary of the whole course
                                                           in its first five chapters; excellent the week before
                                                           an exam.
[Fe]   Feller, An Introduction to Probability Theory and   The classic treatment of combinatorics, discrete
       Its Applications, Vol. I, 3rd ed. (1968)            probability, the binomial and Poisson laws, and
                                                           the normal approximation. Discrete only.



Notes on the PDF copies you have
[F] Your file is the Pearson Custom Library printing (2014). Printed page = PDF page −5
throughout. Theorems, definitions, examples, and exercises are numbered within each chapter. In
this printing some exercises read “intentionally omitted” (e.g. Ch. 4: 12–15, 43–45, 56–59), and the
odd-answer keys were not re-aligned, so a few entries are misprinted; the problem tables below flag
every one that affects a recommended exercise.
[BT] This PDF is the Fall 2000 MIT lecture-note draft of the book: page numbers restart in every
chapter (cited as Ch. c, p. n) and it has no end-of-chapter problems, so the practice set is the book’s
worked Examples. Each is solved immediately after its statement: cover the solution, attempt it,
then compare.
[CB] Second edition (Duxbury, 2002). PDF page = printed page +27 for pp. 1–45 and +26 from
p. 47 on (one blank page is missing from the scan). The book prints no answer key: only Exercises
1.16–1.18 and 1.20–1.24 give their answers, and “show that” problems state the target. Exercises
get harder as their numbers rise, so the low numbers in each chapter suit this course best.
[W] Printed page = PDF page −17. Exercises are cited as Ch. c #n (the exercise list at the end of
chapter c); the book prints no answers, but “show that” problems state their result, and the tables
on pp. 53, 58, and 433 check most means, variances, and MGFs. The PDF has no normal table; use
Freund’s.
[Fe] Your file is the 3rd edition (1968, revised printing), not the 2nd edition its name suggests.
Printed page = PDF page −18. Problems are cited as “V.8 #6” (Chapter V, §8, problem 6);
answers to selected problems are on pp. 483–498 (PDF 501–516). Volume I treats discrete sample
spaces only, so it has no densities, joint PDFs, or Jacobians.

Page references. “p. 82” is the page number printed in the book; “PDF 87” is the page in your
PDF file, so you can jump straight there.


Problem tables. ★ marks an essential problem (do these first). A superscript a means the book
prints the answer (for [BT], the worked solution follows the example), and s means the problem
states the result you are to prove, so you can check yourself. Problems that are essentially ones
you already did on a homework are listed separately under homework connections. Levels: E
routine, M exam-level, H harder than an exam question.

What Midterm I tells you about Midterm II
Midterm I had three common problems (25, 25, and 30 points) and one STA 431 only problem
(20 points). Every problem was a close relative of a homework problem: Problem 3 (f (x) = cx2
on 0 < x < 3; find c, the CDF, and a probability) was Homework 3, Problem 5 almost word for
word, with P (1 < X ≤ 2) in place of P (Y > 2). Expect Midterm II to be built the same way from
Homeworks 4 and 5:
▶ Homework 4: constants, marginals, conditionals, and independence for joint PMFs and PDFs;
  probabilities of regions such as {X < Y } and {X + Y ≤ 3}; the “device fails if either component
  fails” word problem.
▶ Homework 5: means and variances; MGFs, including the condition on t, moments from
  derivatives, and M(X−a)/b ; variances of linear combinations from given variances and covariances;
  product moments and covariance from a joint PMF; conditional means and variances.

If you can do every Homework 4 and 5 problem cold, with correct limits of integration and without
notes, you are in good shape. Mock Exams 4–6 and the Mock Midterm II (Part IV) are written to
that blueprint.




                                                 vii
STA 431 · Study Guide                                                         How to Use This Guide


A study plan for Midterm II
 Day 1. Unit 4 core concepts and worked examples; redo Homework 4 without notes; sketch every
        support region.
 Day 2. Unit 5; redo Homework 5, Problems 1–4. Memorise the MGF transformation rules and
        the ln M (t) shortcut.
 Day 3. Unit 6; redo Homework 5, Problems 5–8. Practise the covariance expansion for linear
        combinations until it is automatic.
 Day 4. Mock Exams 4, 5, and 6, timed at 75 minutes each. Grade them strictly against the
        solutions and rework every lost point.
 Day 5. Mock Midterm II under real conditions. Spend the rest of the day on the problems you
        missed and the pitfalls boxes.


Notation
The guide uses Freund’s notation, so it matches lecture and the text. A few equivalents you will see
in the other books:

Quantity                      Freund (this guide)                      Elsewhere
PMF / PDF of X                f (x)                                    pX (x), fX (x)
CDF (distribution function)   F (x) = P (X ≤ x)                        FX (x)
Marginals of (X, Y )          g(x), h(y)                               fX (x), fY (y)
Conditionals                  f (x | y) = f (x, y)/h(y),               fX|Y , fY |X
                              w(y | x) = f (x, y)/g(x)
Moments                       µ′r = E(X r ), µr = E[(X − µ)r ]         E X r , central moments
Variance, covariance          σ 2 = Var(X), σXY = Cov(X, Y )           var(X), cov(X, Y )
Moment-generating function    MX (t) = E(etX )                         transform, M (s)
Named families                b(x; n, θ), p(x; λ), n(x; µ, σ), . . .   Bin(n, p), Poisson(λ), N(µ, σ 2 )


Freund parametrises the exponential and gamma densities by their scale (θ or β, so the mean is θ
or αβ); Bertsekas–Tsitsiklis and Wasserman often use the rate λ = 1/θ. Always check which one a
problem uses.

               Foundations
Combinatorics, probability, and single random variables

                   Midterm I material




                           1
Unit 1

Combinatorial Methods

    Midterm I · Final        Primary reading: Freund Ch. 1, §§1–3 (pp. 1–17)

   After this unit you should be able to
    ▶ apply the multiplication rule to multi-step operations;
    ▶ count permutations of distinct objects, of objects taken r at a time, of objects in a circle, and of
      objects with repeated types;
    ▶ count combinations, and partitions into labelled or unlabelled groups;
    ▶ handle “at least” and “adjacent” conditions without over-counting;
    ▶ use binomial and multinomial coefficients, and prove identities algebraically, by calculus, and by a
      story (combinatorial) proof.



1.1 Reading guide

Primary: Freund, Chapter 1, Introduction (Combinatorial Methods)
▶ §2 Combinatorial Methods, pp. 2–11    (PDF 7–16)
▶ §3 Binomial Coefficients, pp. 11–17 (PDF 16–22)


§4 The Theory in Practice (pp. 17–18) is optional.

▶ Ex. 11 (p. 7): permutations of the letters of “book”          of 2, 1, 1

   Degas (repeated types)                                       two double rooms

   cist

      text right after Theorem 11 (p. 13).

Supplementary readings
[BT] §1.6 Counting, Ch. 1, pp. 41–48 (PDF 47–54).
Derives every counting formula from one stage-by-stage principle, and obtains the multinomial coefficient two
ways, including the “subscript the repeated letters” trick for anagrams.



                                                          2
STA 431 · Study Guide                                                                             Combinatorial Methods


[CB] §1.2.3 Counting, pp. 13–16 (PDF 40–43).
Derives all four cells of the ordered/unordered, with/without replacement table, including a “markers and
walls” (stars-and-bars) derivation of n+r−1
                                            
                                         r    .
[CB] §1.2.4 Enumerating Outcomes, pp. 16–19 (PDF 43–46).
Poker-hand counts, and why unordered outcomes drawn with replacement are not equally likely.
[Fe] II §4 Subpopulations and Partitions, pp. 34–37 (PDF 52–55).
Derives nr , the arrangements of p alphas and q betas, and multinomial coefficients by “label, then un-label”:
           

the cleanest intuition for repeated-letter problems.
[Fe] II §5 Occupancy Problems (starred), pp. 38–39 (PDF 56–57).
                                       n+r−1
                                             

1.2 Core concepts

         Theorem 1.1. (Multiplication rule) If an operation consists of k steps, the first of which
         can be done in n1 ways, and for each of these the second in n2 ways, and so on, then the
         whole operation can be done in n1 n2 · · · nk ways. [Freund Ch. 1, Thms. 1–2]

The key phrase is “for each of these”: the number of choices at a step may not depend on which
choices were made earlier, even though the choices themselves may.

         Theorem 1.2. (Permutations)

          (i) n distinct objects can be arranged in a row in n! ways. [Freund Thm. 3]
                                                              n!
         (ii) n distinct objects taken r at a time: n Pr =          = n(n − 1) · · · (n − r + 1). [Freund
                                                           (n − r)!
             Thm. 4]
     (iii) n distinct objects arranged in a circle: (n − 1)! (only relative positions matter). [Freund
             Thm. 5]
     (iv) n objects of which n1 are of one kind, n2 of a second kind, . . . , nk of a kth kind:
                 n!
                               . [Freund Thm. 6]
          n1 ! n2 ! · · · nk !


         Theorem 1.3. (Combinations and partitions)

1.3 Worked examples

  Example 1.1 (Repeated letters and adjacency) How many distinct arrangements are
  there of the letters of MISSISSIPPI? In how many of them are all four S’s together?
  Solution. The word has 11 letters: M once, I four times, S four times, P twice. By [Freund
  Thm. 6], the number of arrangements is

                                       11!        39,916,800
                                                =            = 34,650.
                                    1! 4! 4! 2!      1152
  For the S’s to be together, glue them into a single block [SSSS]. We now arrange 8 units: the
                                            8!
  block, M, four I’s, and two P’s, giving        = 840. The block’s internal order adds no factor
                                           4! 2!
  because its letters are identical. A block of k distinct letters would contribute a factor k! (for
  example 2! for a block B1 B2 ).                                                                 ■


  Example 1.2 (“At least” without over-counting) A delegation of 6 is chosen from 7
  juniors and 5 seniors. How many delegations contain at least 2 juniors and at least 2 seniors?
  Solution. Split by the number of juniors j; the seniors are then 6 − j. We need j ≥ 2 and
  6 − j ≥ 2, so j ∈ {2, 3, 4}:
                       
                 7 5           7 5     7 5
                          +        +           = 21 · 5 + 35 · 10 + 35 · 10 = 805 .
                 2 4           3 3     4 2

 The tempting  wrong
                    answer. “Pick 2 juniors, pick 2 seniors, then pick any 2 of the remaining
 8” gives 72 52 82 = 5880. It counts each delegation several times. A delegation with juniors
 {a, b, c} is produced once with {a, b} as the “required” pair and again with {a, c} and with
 {b, c}. Whenever a condition says “at least”, split into disjoint cases or use the complement.■


  Example 1.3 (Labelled versus unlabelled groups) Nine students are split into three
  study groups of three. Count the splits if (a) the groups meet on Monday, Wednesday, and



                                                    5
STA 431 · Study Guide                                                            Combinatorial Methods


  Friday (labelled); (b) the groups are simply “three groups” (unlabelled).
                         
                     9           9!
  Solution. (a)             =          = 1680. (b) Each unlabelled split corresponds to 3! = 6
                  3, 3, 3     3! 3! 3!
  labelled ones, so 1680/6 = 280.                                                           ■

  then set x = 1.
  Story. Count the pairs (committee, chair) that can be formed fromPn people,
                                                                           where a committee
  may have any size r ≥ 1. Choosing the committee first gives r r nr , since there are nr
  committees of size r and r ways to pick each one’s chair. Choosing the chair first gives n ways,
  after which each of the other n − 1 people is either on the committee or not, for n 2n−1 . ■


1.4 Pitfalls and exam strategy

  Common pitfalls
   ▶ Order or no order?        Officers (president, treasurer) are ordered; committees are not.
     Passwords and PINs are ordered. Card hands are not.
   ▶ Over-counting “at least” conditions by pre-selecting the required members (Exam-
     ple 1.2). Use disjoint cases or the complement.
   ▶ Dividing by k! is only for groups that are both unlabelled and of equal size. Groups of
     sizes 3, 4, 5 are automatically distinguishable.
   ▶ Adjacency: treat the adjacent items as one block, then multiply by the block’s internal
     arrangements (1 if the items are identical).
   ▶ Multinomial coefficients carry the numerical coefficients and signs of each term, e.g.
     (2x − y + 3z)7 contributes 2a (−1)b 3c .


  Exam strategy
  Write one sentence describing the process that builds each object (“choose the 3 positions
  for the T’s, then . . . ”), then multiply the stage counts. If you cannot describe a process that


                                                  6
STA 431 · Study Guide                                                                    Combinatorial Methods


   produces every object exactly once, the count is wrong. For a probability, check that the
   numerator and denominator count the same kind of object, both ordered or both unordered.

1. (10 pts) Consider the letters of the word STATISTICS.

Unit 2

Probability

    Midterm I · Final       Primary reading: Freund Ch. 2, §§1–8 (pp. 21–52)

  After this unit you should be able to
   ▶ describe sample spaces and translate verbal events into unions, intersections, and complements;
   ▶ use the axioms and their consequences, including inclusion–exclusion for two and three events;
   ▶ compute equally-likely probabilities by counting;
   ▶ compute and interpret conditional probabilities and use the multiplication rule;
   ▶ decide whether events are independent, and use independence (series/parallel reliability);
   ▶ apply the rule of total probability and Bayes’ theorem, especially in “which source?” problems.

STA 431 · Study Guide                                                                              Probability


Treats P (· | B) as a new probability law on a shrunken sample space; sequential trees with the multiplication
rule become the standard modelling tool.
[BT] §1.4 Total Probability Theorem and Bayes’ Rule, Ch. 1, pp. 25–31 (PDF 31–37).
Total probability as divide-and-conquer, Bayes as reasoning from an effect back to its cause, both drawn as
trees. (The PDF’s table of contents swaps the titles of §§1.4 and 1.5.)
[BT] §1.5 Independence, Ch. 1, pp. 31–39 (PDF 37–45).
Conditional versus unconditional independence, pairwise versus mutual independence, and series/parallel
reliability.
[CB] §1.2.2 The Calculus of Probabilities, pp. 9–13 (PDF 36–40).
Every rule proved from the axioms, plus Bonferroni’s and Boole’s inequalities.
[CB] §1.3 Conditional Probability and Independence, pp. 20–27 (PDF 47–54).
The three-prisoners problem (Example 1.3.4) is a superb Bayes intuition-builder; also a noisy-channel example
and pairwise versus mutual independence.
[Fe] II §3, Example (d): the birthday problem, p. 33 (PDF 51).
With 23 people a shared birthday is more likely than not; the approximation log p ≈ −r(r − 1)/730 shows why.
[Fe] IV §1 Union of Events, pp. 98–101 (PDF 116–119).
General inclusion–exclusion proved by counting, then applied to the classical matching problem, where the
chance of at least one match is about 1 − 1/e.
[Fe] V §1 Conditional Probability and V §3 Stochastic Independence, pp. 114–118, 125–128
(PDF 132–136, 143–146).
Conditioning as shrinking the sample space, the chain rule, Bayes for strata, and why mutual independence of
n events needs 2n − n − 1 equations.
[W] §§1.2–1.3 Sample Spaces and Events; Probability, pp. 3–7 (PDF 20–24).
A one-table dictionary of event algebra and the axioms, with a clean proof of the addition rule.
[W] §§1.5–1.6 Independent Events; Conditional Probability, pp. 8–12 (PDF 25–29).
Two quick worked examples (a geometric-series basketball game; a “90%-accurate” test with P (D | +) ≈ 0.08)
and boxed lists of the classic errors.
[W] §1.7 Bayes’ Theorem, pp. 12–13 (PDF 29–30).
Total probability and Bayes proved in two lines, applied to a three-category spam filter. (Misprint in Exam-
ple 1.19: the third likelihood should read P (B | A3 ).)


2.2 Core concepts

2.2.1 Sample spaces, events, and set algebra
The sample space S is the set of all possible outcomes. An event is a subset of S, and A and B are
mutually exclusive if A ∩ B = ∅. [Freund Ch. 2, Defs. 1–3]




                                                     12
STA 431 · Study Guide                                                                             Probability


                 In words                  In symbols
                 A or B (at least one)     A∪B
                 A and B (both)            A∩B
                 not A                     A′ (Freund writes A′ ; also Ac , Ā)
                 only A (of A, B, C)       A ∩ B′ ∩ C ′
                 exactly one of A, B, C    (A ∩ B ′ ∩ C ′ ) ∪ (A′ ∩ B ∩ C ′ ) ∪ (A′ ∩ B ′ ∩ C)
                 at least one of A, B, C   A∪B∪C
                 none of A, B, C           A′ ∩ B ′ ∩ C ′ = (A ∪ B ∪ C)′


De Morgan’s laws, (A∪B)′ = A′ ∩B ′ and (A∩B)′ = A′ ∪B ′ , turn “none” and “not both” statements
into complements.

2.2.2 Axioms and rules

  Definition 2.1. (Postulates of probability) (1) P (A) ≥ 0 for every event A; (2) P (S) = 1;
  (3) if A1 , A2 , . . . are mutually exclusive, P (A1 ∪ A2 ∪ · · · ) = P (A1 ) + P (A2 ) + · · · . [Freund
  Ch. 2, §4]


  Theorem 2.2. (Consequences of the postulates)

   (i) Equally likely outcomes: if S has N equally likely outcomes and A contains n of them,
       then P (A) = n/N . [Freund Thm. 2]
  (ii) P (A′ ) = 1 − P (A); P (∅) = 0; A ⊂ B ⇒ P (A) ≤ P (B); 0 ≤ P (A) ≤ 1. [Freund
      Thms. 3–6]
  (iii) Addition rule: P (A ∪ B) = P (A) + P (B) − P (A ∩ B). [Freund Thm. 7]
  (iv) Inclusion–exclusion: P (A ∪ B ∪ C) = P (A) + P (B) + P (C) − P (A ∩ B) − P (A ∩ C) −
        P (B ∩ C) + P (A ∩ B ∩ C). [Freund Thm. 8]


2.2.3 Conditional probability and independence

  Definition 2.3. (Conditional probability) If P (A) ̸= 0, the conditional probability of B
                         P (A ∩ B)
  given A is P (B | A) =           . [Freund Def. 4]
                           P (A)

Conditioning on A shrinks the sample space to A: probabilities are recomputed as fractions of A.

  Theorem 2.4. (Multiplication rule) P (A ∩ B) = P (A) P (B | A), and more generally
  P (A1 ∩ · · · ∩ An ) = P (A1 )P (A2 | A1 )P (A3 | A1 ∩ A2 ) · · · P (An | A1 ∩ · · · ∩ An−1 ). [Freund
  Thms. 9–10]


  Definition 2.5. (Independence) A and B are independent if and only if P (A ∩ B) =
  P (A)P (B); equivalently, when P (A) > 0, P (B | A) = P (B). Events A1 , . . . , Ak are indepen-


                                                     13
STA 431 · Study Guide                                                                            Probability


   dent if the probability of the intersection of any 2, 3, . . . , or k of them equals the product of
   their probabilities. [Freund Defs. 5–6]

If A and B are independent, so are A and B ′ , A′ and B, and A′ and B ′ . [Freund Thm. 11]

   Independent is not the same as mutually exclusive
   If P (A) > 0 and P (B) > 0 and A, B are mutually exclusive, then P (A ∩ B) = 0 ̸= P (A)P (B),
   so they are dependent: learning that A happened tells you B did not.


2.2.4 Total probability and Bayes’ theorem

  Theorem 2.6. (Rule of total probability; Bayes’ theorem) If B1 , . . . , Bk partition S
  (mutually exclusive, union S) and each P (Bi ) > 0, then for any event A
                  k
                  X                                          P (Br ) P (A | Br )
        P (A) =         P (Bi ) P (A | Bi ),   P (Br | A) = Pk                         (P (A) > 0).
                  i=1                                        i=1 P (Bi ) P (A | Bi )

   [Freund Thms. 12–13]

Read Bayes’ theorem as “the share of A that came through branch r”: draw a tree with the Bi on
the first level and A on the second, multiply along branches, and divide the branch of interest by
the total.

  Theorem 2.7. Q (Reliability) Independent components with reliabilities
                                                                     Q R1 , . . . , Rn : series
  system Rs = i Ri (works only if all work); parallel system Rp = 1 − i (1 − Ri ) (works if at
  least one works). [Freund Def. 7, Thms. 14–15]


   Key formulas
   Complement                            P (A′ ) = 1 − P (A)
   Union                                 P (A ∪ B) = P (A) + P (B) − P (A ∩ B)
   Conditional                           P (B | A) = P (A ∩ B)/P (A)
   Independence                          P (A ∩ B) = P (A)P (B)
                                             Q               
   “At least one” (independent)          1 − i 1 − P (Ai )
                                                  P
   Total probability                     P (A) = i P (Bi )P (A | Bi )
                                                                        
   Bayes                                 P (Br | A) = P (Br )P (A | Br ) P (A)

2.3 Worked examples

  Example 2.1 (Two-way table) Of 400 students, 240 live on campus. A car is owned by 150
  of the on-campus students and 60 of the off-campus students. A student is chosen at random.
  Find P (car) and P (on campus | car), and decide whether car ownership is independent of
  residence.
  Solution. Build the table first:
                                                  Car   No car   Total
                                     On campus    150     90     240
                                     Off campus   60     100     160
                                     Total        210    190     400


  P (car) = 210/400 = 0.525, and P (on | car) = 150/210 = 5/7 ≈ 0.714. Since P (car | on) =
  150/240 = 0.625 ̸= 0.525 = P (car), the events are not independent.                     ■


  Example 2.2 (Bayes: did the student know it?) On a 4-option multiple-choice question
  a student knows the answer with probability 0.7; otherwise she guesses at random. Given that
  she answered correctly, what is the probability that she knew the answer?
  Solution. Let K = knew, C = correct. Then P (C | K) = 1 and P (C | K ′ ) = 1/4, so by total
                                                                         0.7    28
  probability P (C) = 0.7(1) + 0.3(0.25) = 0.775. By Bayes, P (K | C) =       =    ≈ 0.903 .■
                                                                        0.775   31


  Example 2.3 (Complement for “at least one”) Find the probability of at least one six
  in four rolls of a fair die.
  Solution. The rolls are independent, so P (no six) = (5/6)4 and P (at least one six) = 1 −
  (5/6)4 ≈ 0.5177.                                                                         ■


  Example 2.4 (Pairwise does not imply mutual independence) Toss two fair coins. Let
  A = first is heads, B = second is heads, C = exactly one head. Show that A, B, C are pairwise
  independent but not independent.
  Solution. Each event has probability 1/2. Then P (A ∩ B) = P (HH) = 1/4, P (A ∩ C) =
  P (HT ) = 1/4, and P (B ∩ C) = P (T H) = 1/4, so each pair is independent. But A ∩ B ∩ C = ∅,
  so P (A ∩ B ∩ C) = 0 ̸= 1/8.                                                               ■




                                                   15
STA 431 · Study Guide                                                                   Probability


2.4 Pitfalls and exam strategy

   Common pitfalls
    ▶ P (A | B) versus P (B | A). Read which event is given. Bayes’ theorem converts one into
      the other.
    ▶ Wrong denominator. P (Y | X ′ ) divides by P (X ′ ) = 1 − P (X), not by P (X).
    ▶ Mutually exclusive ̸= independent (see the note above).
    ▶ Adding probabilities of overlapping events without subtracting the intersection.
    ▶ Independence assumed from the story (“different machines”) when the problem gives
      numbers that contradict it. Always test P (A ∩ B) = P (A)P (B).


   Exam strategy
   For every Bayes problem: (1) name the events with letters; (2) list the given probabilities,
   noting which are conditional ; (3) draw the tree; (4) compute the total P (A) first. Part (a)
   of such a problem is usually P (A), and you need it for part (b) anyway. For counting-based
   probabilities, make sure numerator and denominator count the same kind of object, both
   ordered or both unordered.

2.6 Mock Exam 2

  Mock Exam 2: Probability                                                         Solutions → p. 102
  Time: 75 minutes · Total: 100 points          · Show all work; simplify exact answers; a scientific
  calculator is allowed.


1. (8 pts) Let A, B, C be events. Write set expressions for the events that
    (a) at least two of the three events occur;
    (b) exactly two occur;
    (c) none occurs;
    (d) at most one occurs.

2. (10 pts) Suppose P (A) = 0.5, P (B) = 0.4, and P (A ∩ B) = 0.15. Find

    (a) P (A ∪ B)                      (c) P (A′ ∩ B ′ )                 (e) P (B | A′ )
    (b) P (A ∩ B ′ )                   (d) P (A | B)                     (f) Are A, B independent?

3. (10 pts) Of 200 students, 90 take Calculus, 70 take Physics, and 60 take Chemistry; 30 take
   Calculus and Physics, 25 Calculus and Chemistry, and 20 Physics and Chemistry; 10 take all
   three.
    (a) How many take none of the three?
    (b) How many take exactly one?
    (c) A student is chosen at random. Find P (Physics | Calculus).

4. (10 pts) A 5-card hand is dealt from a standard deck. Find the probability that it contains
    (a) exactly two aces;
    (b) at least one ace;
    (c) five cards of the same suit.

5. (10 pts) Assume 365 equally likely birthdays and no twins.
    (a) Write a formula for the probability that, among n people, at least two share a birthday.
    (b) Evaluate it for n = 23.

6. (12 pts) Machines A, B, and C produce 50%, 30%, and 20% of a factory’s output, with defect
   rates 2%, 3%, and 5%, respectively.
    (a) Find the probability that a randomly selected item is defective.
    (b) Given that an item is defective, find the probability that machine C produced it.
    (c) Given that an item is not defective, find the probability that machine A produced it.

7. (10 pts) A disease has prevalence 2%. A screening test has sensitivity P (+ | D) = 0.95 and
   specificity P (− | D′ ) = 0.90.
    (a) Find P (+).
    (b) Find P (D | +) and explain in one sentence why it is so small.
    (c) Find P (D | −).

Unit 3

Random Variables: Distributions and Densities

    Midterm I · Final      Primary reading: Freund Ch. 3, §§1–4 (pp. 61–81)

   After this unit you should be able to
   ▶ define a random variable and find the probability distribution (PMF) of a discrete one from an
      experiment;
   ▶ move between a PMF and its distribution function (CDF), and sketch both;
   ▶ find the normalising constant of a density, and the CDF of a (possibly piecewise) density;
   ▶ compute probabilities and percentiles from a PMF, a PDF, or a CDF;
   ▶ recognise when a proposed function cannot be a PMF, PDF, or CDF.

3.2 Core concepts

   Definition 3.1. (Random variable) A random variable X is a real-valued function defined
   on the sample space S. It is discrete if its range is finite or countably infinite, and continuous
   if probabilities are given by integrating a density. [Freund Ch. 3, Def. 1]


3.2.1 Discrete random variables

   Definition 3.2. (PMF and CDF of a discrete random variable) The probability
   distribution of a P                                                               P if f (x) ≥ 0
                     discrete X is f (x) = P (X = x). A function f is a PMF if and only
   for every x and x f (x) = 1. The distribution function is F (x) = P (X ≤ x) = t≤x f (t) for
   −∞ < x < ∞. [Freund Defs. 2–3, Thm. 1]


   Theorem 3.3. (Properties of a CDF; recovering the PMF) F (−∞) = 0, F (∞) = 1,
   F is non-decreasing, and F is continuous from the right. If the range of X is x1 < x2 < · · · ,
   then f (x1 ) = F (x1 ) and f (xi ) = F (xi ) − F (xi−1 ): the PMF is the size of the jumps of the
   CDF. [Freund Thms. 2–3]


3.2.2 Continuous random variables

   Definition 3.4. (Probability density function) f is a probability density of X if P (a ≤
            Rb
   X ≤Rb) = a f (x) dx for all real a ≤ b. A function can serve as a density if f (x) ≥ 0 for all x
        ∞
   and −∞ f (x) dx = 1. [Freund Def. 4, Thm. 5]



                                                     22
STA 431 · Study Guide                                     Random Variables: Distributions and Densities



  Theorem
  Rx          3.5. (CDF of a continuous random variable) F (x) = P (X ≤ x) =
   −∞ f (t) dt, and f (x) = F ′ (x) wherever the derivative exists. Since P (X = c) = 0 for every c,

         P (a < X < b) = P (a ≤ X < b) = P (a < X ≤ b) = P (a ≤ X ≤ b) = F (b) − F (a).

   [Freund Thm. 4, Def. 5, Thm. 6]


   Densities are not probabilities
   f (x) is a probability per unit length. It can exceed 1 (e.g. f (x) = 3x2 near x = 1), and only
   areas under it are probabilities. A CDF, by contrast, always lies in [0, 1].


3.2.3 The standard four-step problem
Almost every Unit 3 exam question is a version of this (Midterm I, Problem 3; Homework 3,
Problems 2–6):
                     R
 1. Constant: solve support f (x) dx = 1 for c.
                                                                           Rx
 2. CDF on the whole real line: F (x) = 0 to the left of the support, left end f (t) dt inside it,
    and 1 to the right of it. For a piecewise density, add the area already accumulated at each
    breakpoint.
 3. Probabilities: P (a < X ≤ b) = F (b) − F (a) and P (X > a) = 1 − F (a).
 4. Percentiles: the 100pth percentile solves F (xp ) = p; the median solves F (m) = 12 .

   Key formulas
                    Discrete                                  Continuous
                                P                                          R
   Validity         f (x) ≥ 0,     f (x) = 1                  f (x) ≥ 0, f (x) dx = 1
                            P x                                       Rx
   CDF              F (x) = t≤x f (t) (step function)         F (x) = −∞ f (t) dt (continuous)
   Back to f        jump sizes of F                           f (x) = F ′ (x)
   P (a < X ≤ b)    F (b) − F (a)                             F (b) − F (a); endpoints irrelevant
   P (X = c)        f (c)                                     0



3.3 Worked examples

  Example 3.1 (A PMF and its step-function CDF) Two fair dice are rolled; M is the
  larger of the two numbers. Find the PMF and CDF of M and sketch the CDF.
  Solution. M ≤ k means both dice are ≤ k, so F (k) = P (M ≤ k) = (k/6)2 = k 2 /36 for
  k = 1, . . . , 6. The jumps give the PMF:

  Example 3.3 (From a CDF to a density, a probability, and the median) F (x) =
  1 − (1 + x)−2 for x > 0 and F (x) = 0 otherwise. Find the density, P (X > 1), and the median.
  Solution. f (x) = F ′ (x) = 2(1 + x)−3 for x > 0. Next, P (X > 1) = 1 − F (1) = (1 + 1)−2 = 14 .
                                                                  √
  For the median, 1 − (1 + m)−2 = 21 gives (1 + m)2 = 2, so m = 2 − 1 ≈ 0.414.               ■




                                                                   24
STA 431 · Study Guide                                    Random Variables: Distributions and Densities


  Example 3.4 (Is it a density?) Can f (x) = c(x − 1) for 0 < x < 3 (zero elsewhere) be a
  density for some constant c?
                                            R3
  Solution. Normalisation alone would give 0 (x − 1) dx = 32 , so c = 23 . But then f (x) < 0 for
  0 < x < 1, which violates f ≥ 0. No constant works. Always check both conditions.            ■



3.4 Pitfalls and exam strategy

   Common pitfalls
    ▶ Incomplete CDF. F must be given for every real x: include F (x) = 0 to the left of the
      support and F (x) = 1 to the right.
    ▶ Restarting the area at a breakpoint. On the second piece of a piecewise density, F
      starts from the area already accumulated (Example 3.2).
    ▶ Strict versus non-strict inequalities. Irrelevant for continuous X, crucial for discrete
      X: P (X < 3) = F (3) − f (3) when X is discrete.
    ▶ Using f (b) − f (a) instead of F (b) − F (a) for an interval probability.
    ▶ “Exactly 1 minute early” for a continuous variable has probability 0 (Homework 3,
      Problem 4(c)).


   Exam strategy
   Box the constant, write the CDF as a cases display covering (−∞, ∞), and check F : it must
   be continuous for a density, start at 0, end at 1, and never decrease. When asked to sketch,
   label the axes, the breakpoints, and the heights, and draw open and closed dots at jumps.

Midterm I Revisited: Model Solutions
Midterm I is the best predictor of how your remaining exams will be written and graded, so here it
is solved in full. Use it two ways: as a final check on Units 1–3, and as a model of how much work
to show. Point values are those on the exam.

Problem 1 (25 pts) Passwords
A password consists of exactly 5 characters chosen from the 26 uppercase letters and 10 digits. (a)
How many distinct passwords are there if repetition is not allowed? (b) If a password is generated
at random without repetition, what is the exact probability that it contains exactly 3 letters and 2
digits?
(a) There are 36 symbols, and order matters with no repetition:

                             36 P5 = 36 · 35 · 34 · 33 · 32 =   45,239,040 .

(b) Ordered count. Choose which 3 of the 5 positions hold letters ( 53 = 10 ways), fill them with
                                                                             

distinct letters (26 · 25 · 24), and fill the other two with distinct digits (10 · 9):
                             5
                              
                             3 · 26 · 25 · 24 · 10 · 9   14,040,000   1625
                     P =                               =            =       ≈ 0.3104.
                              36 · 35 · 34 · 33 · 32     45,239,040   5236

Unordered check. The set of 5 symbols is equally likely to be any of the 36
                                                                                 
                                                                               5   sets, so P =
 26 10
       
 3         2600 · 45   1625
   36
      2 =           =       . Both methods agree; mixing them (ordered numerator over unordered
    5
           376,992     5236
denominator) is the classic error.

Problem 2 (25 pts) Two machines
Machine A makes 60% of the components and B makes 40%; 3% of A’s and 5% of B’s are defective.
(a) Find the probability that a random component is defective. (b) Given that a component is
defective, find the probability that A made it.
Let D = defective. (a) By total probability, P (D) = P (A)P (D | A) + P (B)P (D | B) = 0.6(0.03) +
0.4(0.05) = 0.018 + 0.020 = 0.038 .
                                    0.018       9
(b) By Bayes’ theorem, P (A | D) =         =       ≈ 0.474. Machine A makes most of the parts, but
                                    0.038      19
its lower defect rate means it accounts for slightly less than half of the defectives.

Problem 4 (20 pts, STA 431 only) Independent events
A and B are independent with P (A) = 0.4 and P (B) = 0.5. (a) Calculate P (A ∪ B) and P (A ∩ B ′ ).
(b) Let Y be the number of the events A, B that occur. Find the PMF of Y .
(a) P (A ∩ B) = P (A)P (B) = 0.2, so P (A ∪ B) = 0.4 + 0.5 − 0.2 = 0.7 . Since A and B ′ are also
independent, P (A ∩ B ′ ) = 0.4(0.5) = 0.2 .
(b) P (Y = 0) = P (A′ ∩ B ′ ) = 0.6(0.5) = 0.3; P (Y = 2) = P (A ∩ B) = 0.2; P (Y = 1) =
P (A ∩ B ′ ) + P (A′ ∩ B) = 0.2 + 0.3 = 0.5.
                                 
                                 0.3, y = 0,
                                 
                          f (y) = 0.5, y = 1,   and 0.3 + 0.5 + 0.2 = 1.
                                 
                                   0.2, y = 2,
                                 


   What this exam rewards

    ▶ Each problem was a homework problem in new clothes. Problem 3 is Homework 3,
      Problem 5 (same density, same domain). Problem 2 is Homework 2, Problem 4 with two
      sources instead of three.
    ▶ Final answers were short; set-ups carried the points. Write the formula you are
      using (e.g. P (A | D) = P (A)P (D | A)/P (D)) before substituting numbers.
    ▶ Part (a) usually feeds part (b). The constant feeds the CDF, which feeds the probability;
      P (D) feeds Bayes.

Unit 4

Multivariate Distributions

    Midterm II · Final       Primary reading: Freund Ch. 3, §§5–7 (pp. 82–101)

  After this unit you should be able to
   ▶ find the constant that makes a joint PMF or joint PDF valid;
   ▶ derive marginal distributions, with the correct limits when the support is not a rectangle;
   ▶ derive conditional distributions and use them to compute conditional probabilities;
   ▶ decide whether random variables are independent, and justify the answer;
   ▶ turn a word problem into a region of the plane and set up the integral over it;
   ▶ move between a joint CDF and a joint density.

4.2 Core concepts

4.2.1 Joint distributions

   Definition 4.1. (Joint probability distribution (discrete)) f (x,        Py) P
                                                                                = P (X = x, Y = y).
   A function is a joint PMF if and only if f (x, y) ≥ 0 for all (x,
                                                                   P y) and
                                                                         P x y f (x, y) = 1. The
   joint distribution function is F (x, y) = P (X ≤ x, Y ≤ y) = s≤x t≤y f (s, t). [Freund Ch. 3,
   Defs. 6–7, Thm. 7]


   Definition 4.2. (Joint probability density (continuous)) f (x, y) is a joint density of X
   and Y if, for any region A of the xy-plane,
                                                ZZ
                                              
                                P (X, Y ) ∈ A =    f (x, y) dx dy.
                                                          A
                                                                         R∞ R∞
   A function can serve as a joint density if f (x, y) ≥ 0 everywhere and −∞ −∞ f (x, y) dx dy = 1.
                                    Ry Rx                                 ∂ 2 F (x, y)
   The joint CDF is F (x, y) = −∞ −∞ f (s, t) ds dt, and f (x, y) =                    wherever the
                                                                             ∂x ∂y
   derivative exists. [Freund Defs. 8–9, Thm. 8]


4.2.2 Marginal and conditional distributions




                                                     33
STA 431 · Study Guide                                                                       Multivariate Distributions


  Definition 4.3. (Marginal distributions)
                                   X                                            X
                 discrete:  g(x) =    f (x, y),                       h(y) =         f (x, y);
                                                  y                              x
                                                 Z ∞                            Z ∞
                      continuous: g(x) =               f (x, y) dy, h(y) =            f (x, y) dx.
                                                  −∞                             −∞

  To get the marginal of one variable, sum or integrate the other variable over its range, which
  may depend on the variable you keep. [Freund Defs. 10–11]


  Definition 4.4. (Conditional distributions)

                                 f (x, y)                                    f (x, y)
                   f (x | y) =            (h(y) ̸= 0),          w(y | x) =            (g(x) ̸= 0).
                                  h(y)                                        g(x)

  For fixed y, f (x | y) is a genuine PMF or PDF in x: it sums or integrates to 1. Conditional
                                                      Rb
  probabilities come from it: P (a < X < b | Y = y) = a f (x | y) dx. [Freund Defs. 12–13]


  A conditional density is a rescaled slice
  Fix y and look at the slice x 7→ f (x, y) of the joint density. Its area is h(y), so dividing by
  h(y) makes it integrate to 1. The support of f (x | y) is the slice of the support at height y, so
  it can depend on y.


4.2.3 Independence

  Definition 4.5. (Independence of random variables) X1 , . . . , Xn are independent if and
  only if f (x1 , . . . , xn ) = f1 (x1 )f2 (x2 ) · · · fn (xn ) for all (x1 , . . . , xn ), where fi is the marginal of
  Xi . The definition is the same for densities. [Freund Def. 14]


  Theorem 4.6. (Factorisation test) X and Y are independent if and only if the joint PMF
  or PDF can be written f (x, y) = a(x) b(y) for all real x, y, including the points where f = 0.
  In particular:
   ▶ if the support (where f > 0) is not a product set, i.e. a rectangle or a grid, then X and Y
     are dependent;
   ▶ if the support is a rectangle and the formula factors, they are independent, and g ∝ a,
     h ∝ b.

  [Freund follows from Def. 14; see also [CB] Lemma 4.2.7, [W] Thm. 2.33]


  How to justify (in)dependence on an exam

   ▶ Dependent: exhibit one point with f (x, y) ̸= g(x)h(y), e.g. “f (0, 0) = 0 but g(0)h(0) = 1 ”
                                                                                               27



                                                           34
STA 431 · Study Guide                                                             Multivariate Distributions


      in Example 4.1. On a non-rectangular support pick a point outside the support where both
      marginals are positive.
    ▶ Independent: show f (x, y) = g(x)h(y) for every point, either symbolically or with the
      full table. Checking one point, or a single conditional f (x | y0 ), proves nothing.


4.2.4 Setting up the limits of integration
This is where most Midterm II points are lost. Use the same four steps every time.

 1. Draw the support {f > 0}, e.g. a rectangle or the triangle 0 < x < y < 1.
 2. Draw the event A, e.g. {x < y}, {x + y < 1}, or {max(x, y) ≤ t}, and shade A ∩ {support}.
 3. Choose an order. The outer limits are constants: the extent of the shaded region along that
    axis. The inner limits are curves: for a fixed outer value, where the line through the region
    enters and leaves it. If the entering or leaving curve changes partway, split the integral.
 4. Consider the complement if the shaded region is awkward: P (A) = 1 − P (A′ ).


Words                                                     Event                  Typical set-up on (0, 1)2
                                                                                 R 1R 1
first fails before second                                 {X < Y }                       f dy dx
                                                                                 R0c R xc−x
total below c ≤ 1                                         {X + Y < c}             0 0R R

                                                                                         1 1
series system (fails if either fails) by t                {min(X, Y ) ≤ t}       1 − t t f dy dx
                                                                                              R tR t
parallel system (fails if both fail) by t                 {max(X, Y ) ≤ t}       F (t, t) = 0 0 f dy dx
within d of each other                                    {|X − Y | < d}         1− two corner triangles

reimbursed at least a when X = x0                         {Y ≥ a} given X = x0    a

   Key formulas
   Validity                      f ≥ 0, total mass 1
                                          R                    R
   Marginals                     g(x) = f (x, y) dy, h(y) = f (x, y) dx (limits from the support!)
   Conditionals                  f (x | y) = f (x, y)/h(y), w(y | x) = f (x, y)/g(x)
   Independence                  f (x, y) = g(x)h(y) for all (x, y) ⇔ factorisation on a product
                                 support
   Joint CDF → density           f = ∂ 2 F/∂x ∂y
                                                      RR
   Region probability            P ((X, Y ) ∈ A) = A f (x, y) dx dy



4.3 Worked examples

   Example 4.1 (Joint PMF by table) Let f (x, y) = c(x + y) for x ∈ {0, 1, 2}, y ∈ {0, 1}.
   Find c, the marginals, f (x | 1), and P (X > Y ), and decide whether X and Y are independent.
   Solution. The six values of x + y sum to 9, so c = 19 . Lay out the table and add the margins:




                                                     35
STA 431 · Study Guide                                                             Multivariate Distributions



                                                  y=0      y=1       g(x)
                                       x=0          0       1/9      1/9
                                       x=1         1/9      2/9      3/9
                                       x=2         2/9      3/9      5/9
                                       h(y)        3/9      6/9       1

  Divide the y = 1 column by h(1) = 69 : f (0 | 1) = 16 , f (1 | 1) = 26 , f (2 | 1) = 36 . Next,
  P (X > Y ) = f (1, 0)+f (2, 0)+f (2, 1) = 1+2+3
                                              9   = 23 . Finally, f (0, 0) = 0 but g(0)h(0) = 19 · 39 ̸= 0,
  so X and Y are dependent.                                                                             ■

  Example 4.4 (Series versus parallel) A device has two components whose lifetimes X
  and Y (hours) are independent exponentials with means 2 and 3, so f (x, y) = 16 e−x/2−y/3 for
  x, y > 0. Find the probability that the device fails within the first hour if (a) it fails when
  either component fails; (b) it fails only when both have failed.
  Solution. (a) Series: the device’s lifetime is min(X, Y ), and P (min ≤ 1) = 1 − P (X > 1, Y >
  1) = 1 − e−1/2 e−1/3 = 1 − e−5/6 ≈ 0.565 .
  (b) Parallel: the lifetime is max(X, Y ), and P (max ≤ 1) = P (X ≤ 1, Y ≤ 1) = (1 − e−1/2 )(1 −
  e−1/3 ) ≈ 0.112 .
  For a dependent joint density (Homework 4, Problem 3; Mock Exam 4, Problem 5) the set-ups
  are identical; only the double integrals change.                                       ■


  Example 4.5 (Conditional probability from a conditional density) Daily demand X
  (hundreds of units) has density g(x) = x/2 on (0, 2). Given X = x, the amount actually sold,
  Y , is uniform on (0, x). Find the joint density, the marginal of Y , and the probability that
  more than 100 units are sold on a day when demand is 150.
  Solution. Joint = marginal × conditional: f (x, y) = g(x) w(y | x) = x2 · x1 = 12 on 0 < y < x < 2.
                R2
  Then h(y) = y 12 dx = 2−y2 for 0 < y < 2. The question asks for P (Y > 1 | X = 1.5), which




                                                          37
STA 431 · Study Guide                                                             Multivariate Distributions


                                             1
  uses the conditional density w(y | 1.5) = 1.5 on (0, 1.5):
                                                        Z 1.5
                                                                dy
                               P (Y > 1 | X = 1.5) =                = 13 .
                                                         1      1.5

  Integrating the joint density instead of the conditional one here is a common, costly mistake.■



4.4 Pitfalls and exam strategy

   Common pitfalls
                                                                             R1            R1
    ▶ Rectangle limits on a triangle. On 0 < x < y < 1, g(x) =
                                                                             x f dy, not   0 f dy.
    ▶ Missing supports. Every marginal and conditional needs “for . . . , and 0 elsewhere.” A
      conditional’s support may depend on the given value.
    ▶ Joint instead of conditional. P (Y ≥ 8 | X = 12) integrates w(y | 12), not f (12, y).
    ▶ Independence “proved” at one point, or a constant density on a triangle called
      independent.
    ▶ Series/parallel confusion. “Fails if either fails” means the lifetime is the minimum.
    ▶ Discrete sums. For a joint PMF, write out the table. It makes marginals, conditionals,
      and P (X > Y ) mechanical and checkable (rows and columns must add to the margins, and
      the margins to 1).


   Exam strategy
   Draw the support in the margin of every continuous problem, even when it is a rectangle.
   After finding c, check that the marginal you derive integrates to 1; this catches most limit
   errors in seconds. For a region probability, write the integral with its limits before doing any
   algebra. Graders give generous partial credit for a correct set-up.

4.6 Mock Exam 4

  Mock Exam 4: Multivariate Distributions                                        Solutions → p. 105
  Time: 75 minutes · Total: 100 points        · Show all work; simplify exact answers; a scientific
  calculator is allowed.


1. (12 pts) Let f (x, y) = c(2x + y) for x ∈ {0, 1, 2} and y ∈ {0, 1, 2}.
    (a) Find c.
    (b) Find the marginal PMFs g(x) and h(y).
    (c) Find the conditional PMF f (x | 1) and compare it with g(x).
    (d) Are X and Y independent? Justify your answer.
    (e) Find P (X + Y ≤ 2).

2. (10 pts) The joint PMF of X and Y is given by the table.

                                                  y=1     y=2
                                          x=1     0.10    0.20
                                          x=2     0.15    0.25
                                          x=3     0.05    0.25

    (a) Find the marginal PMFs.
    (b) Find P (X > Y ) and P (X = Y ).
    (c) Find the conditional PMF of X given Y = 2.
    (d) Are X and Y independent?
    (e) Find F (2, 1).

3. (14 pts) Let f (x, y) = cxy for 0 < x < y < 1 and 0 elsewhere.
    (a) Find c.
    (b) Find the marginal densities g(x) and h(y), with their supports.
    (c) Find f (x | y) and P X < 12 | Y = 34 .
                                            

    (d) Find P (X + Y < 1).
    (e) Are X and Y independent? Justify your answer.

4. (10 pts) Let f (x, y) = c(x + 2y) for 0 < x < 2, 0 < y < 1, and 0 elsewhere.
    (a) Find c.
    (b) Find g(x) and h(y).
    (c) Find P (X < Y ).
    (d) Find P (X + Y < 1).

5. (10 pts) A device contains two components whose lifetimes X and Y (in hours) have joint
   density f (x, y) = 23 (x + 2y) for 0 < x < 1, 0 < y < 1.
    (a) The device fails if either component fails. Find the probability that it fails during its first
        half hour of operation.
    (b) Repeat part (a) if the device fails only when both components have failed.

STA 431 · Study Guide                                                               Multivariate Distributions

    y > 0, and 0 otherwise.
     (a) Find the joint density.
     (b) Are X and Y independent?
     (c) Find P (X > Y ).
     (d) Find P (X + Y < 1).

 7. (10 pts) Two friends agree to meet between 12:00 and 1:00. Each arrives at a time uniformly
    distributed over the hour, independently of the other, and waits 15 minutes for the other before
    leaving. Set up the problem as a region in the unit square and find the probability that they
    meet.

    each answer in one sentence.
                              2   2
     (a) f (x, y) = 4xy e−(x +y ) for x > 0, y > 0.
     (b) f (x, y) = 2 for 0 < x < y < 1.
     (c) f (x, y) = x + y for 0 < x < 1, 0 < y < 1.

Unit 5

Expectation, Moments, and
Moment-Generating Functions

   Midterm II · Final        Primary reading: Freund Ch. 4, §§1–5 (pp. 113–131)

  After this unit you should be able to
   ▶ compute E(X) and E[g(X)] for discrete and continuous X without first finding the distribution of
      g(X);
   ▶ use linearity, and compute moments about the origin and about the mean, the variance, and (if
      asked) skewness;
   ▶ apply Chebyshev’s theorem and explain why it is a bound, not an estimate;
   ▶ derive a moment-generating function, state where it exists, and get moments from it by differentiation
      or series;
   ▶ use MX+a , MbX , M(X+a)/b and uniqueness to identify distributions.

5.2 Core concepts

5.2.1 Expectation

   Definition 5.1. (Expected value)
                         X                                       Z ∞
               E(X) =         x f (x) (discrete),      E(X) =          x f (x) dx (continuous),
                          x                                       −∞

   provided the sum or integral converges absolutely. Otherwise the mean does not exist; the
   Cauchy distribution is the standard example. [Freund Ch. 4, Def. 1]




                                                       44
STA 431 · Study Guide                                                       Expectation, Moments, and MGFs



   Theorem 5.2. (Expectation of a function (LOTUS))
                           X                        Z ∞
                 E[g(X)] =   g(x)f (x) or E[g(X)] =     g(x)f (x) dx.
                                  x                                    −∞

   You do not need the distribution of g(X). [Freund Thm. 1]

                                                                                        P            
   Theorem
   P          5.3. (Linearity) E(aX + b) = a E(X) + b, E(b) = b, and E                      i ci gi (X)   =

    i i E[gi (X)]. [Freund Thms. 2–3, Cors. 1–2]


Linearity does not extend to non-linear functions: in general E(X 2 ) ̸= [E(X)]2 , E(1/X) ̸= 1/ E(X),
and E(eX ) ̸= eE X .

5.2.2 Moments

   Definition 5.4. (Moments) The rth moment about the origin is µ′r = E(X r ), with µ = µ′1
   the mean. The rth moment about the mean is µr = E[(X − µ)r ]. The variance is σ 2 = µ2 =
   E[(X − µ)2 ], and σ is the standard deviation. [Freund Defs. 2–5]


   Theorem 5.5. (Computing formulas) σ 2 = µ′2 −µ2 = E(X 2 )−[E(X)]2 ,                  and    Var(aX +
   b) = a2 σ 2 . [Freund Thms. 6–7]
   More generally, by expanding (X − µ)3 : µ3 = µ′3 − 3µ µ′2 + 2µ3 . Skewness is α3 = µ3 /σ 3 : it
   is 0 for a symmetric distribution, positive with a long right tail, and negative with a long left
   tail. [Freund Ch. 4 exercises]


                                                                                               1
   Theorem 5.6. (Chebyshev’s theorem) For any k > 0, P (|X − µ| < kσ) ≥ 1 − 2 ,
                                                                                              k
                                     1
   equivalently P (|X − µ| ≥ kσ) ≤ 2 . It holds for every distribution with finite variance, so it
                                     k
   is usually far from sharp. [Freund Thm. 8]

               5.7. (Moment-generating function) MX (t) = E(etX ) =

     e f (x) dx, where it exists. It is useful only when it is finite on an open interval around
   t = 0, and you must state that interval: for f (x) = λe−λx , MX (t) = λ/(λ − t) for t < λ.
   [Freund Def. 6]


   Theorem 5.8. (Moments from the MGF)

  [Freund Thm. 10]. Uniqueness: if two random variables have the same MGF on an interval

  Example 5.4 (MGF of a standardised variable) MX (t) = e3t+2t . Find the MGF of
  Z = (X − 3)/2 and identify its distribution.
                                                             3             3    3    t2     2
  Solution. By Theorem 10 with a = −3, b = 2: MZ (t) = e− 2 t MX 2t = e− 2 t e 2 t+2· 4 = et /2 .
                                                                     

  This is the MGF of the standard normal, so by uniqueness Z ∼ N(0, 1), with E Z = 0 and
                                                     2 2
  Var Z = 1. Equivalently X ∼ N(3, 4): matching eµt+σ t /2 gives µ = 3 and σ 2 /2 = 2.        ■


  Example 5.5 (Chebyshev in both directions) The number of customers per day has
  mean 120 and standard deviation 10, distribution unknown. (a) Give a lower bound for
  P (100 < X < 140). (b) Find an interval centred at 120 that contains X with probability at
  least 0.96.
  Solution. (a) 100 to 140 is k = 2 standard deviations, so P ≥ 1 − 14 = 0.75. (b) 1 − 1/k 2 = 0.96
  gives k = 5, so the interval is 120 ± 50 = (70, 170). For a normal distribution the probability
  in (a) would be about 0.954: Chebyshev is a worst-case guarantee.                              ■



5.4 Pitfalls and exam strategy

  Common pitfalls
   ▶ No domain for the MGF. “MX (t) = 3 ” earns full credit only with “for t < 3”. Say
                      R∞                      3−t
     why: the integral 0 e−(3−t)x dx diverges otherwise.
   ▶ Differentiating, then forgetting t = 0. E X = M ′ (0), not M ′ (t).
   ▶ Product and chain rule slips on MGFs like e2t (1 − 6t)−3 . The ln M shortcut avoids
     them.
   ▶ Variance of a shifted variable. Var(X + b) = Var X; the shift disappears. Var(−X) =
     Var X, not − Var X.
   ▶ E(X 2 ) versus [E(X)]2 , and E(1/X) versus 1/ E X.
   ▶ Chebyshev misread as an approximation. It is a bound; the exact probability is usually
     much larger.


                                                  47
STA 431 · Study Guide                                                    Expectation, Moments, and MGFs


      Exam strategy
      For “derive the MGF”: write the definition, combine exponents (etx e−3x = e−(3−t)x ), state the
      convergence condition, and evaluate. For “use the MGF to find the moments”: differentiate
      twice, evaluate at 0, then compute σ 2 = µ′2 − µ2 . For “MGF of (X − a)/b”: quote Theorem 10,
      substitute, simplify, and read off the mean and variance, by recognising the form or by the
      ln M trick.

Unit 6

Product Moments, Linear Combinations,
and Conditional Expectation

    Midterm II · Final        Primary reading: Freund Ch. 4, §§6–8 (pp. 131–140)

   After this unit you should be able to
    ▶ compute E[g(X, Y )], product moments, the covariance, and the correlation from a joint PMF or
      PDF;
    ▶ explain the relationship between independence and zero covariance, with a counterexample to the
      converse;
    ▶ find the mean and variance of any linear combination, and the covariance of two linear combinations,
      from given moments;
    ▶ compute conditional means and conditional variances from a conditional distribution;
    ▶ (extension) use E[E(X | Y )] = E X and the law of total variance.

                           XX                                                   ZZ

STA 431 · Study Guide                                          Product Moments and Conditional Expectation


  Definition 6.2. (Product moments; covariance; correlation) µ′r,s = E(X r Y s ) and
  µr,s = E[(X − µX )r (Y − µY )s ]. The covariance is σXY = Cov(X, Y ) = µ1,1 = E[(X − µX )(Y −
                                             σXY
  µY )]. The correlation coefficient is ρ =        , with −1 ≤ ρ ≤ 1, and |ρ| = 1 exactly when Y
                                            σX σY
  is a linear function of X. [Freund Defs. 7–9; ρ first appears in Ch. 6, §7]


  Theorem 6.3. (Computing formula) σXY = E(XY ) − E(X) E(Y ). [Freund Thm. 11]


  Covariance algebra
  Cov(X, X) = Var X; Cov(X,PY ) = Cov(Y,  P X); Cov(aX
                                                      P P + b, cY + d) = ac Cov(X, Y ); and
  covariance is bilinear: Cov      a X
                                  i i i ,    b Y
                                            j j j  =    i  j ai bj Cov(Xi , Yj ). Every formula in
  §6.2.3 follows from this one rule. Correlation is unchanged by shifts and positive rescalings:
  Corr(aX + b, cY + d) = sign(ac) Corr(X, Y ).


6.2.2 Independence and covariance

  Theorem 6.4. (Independent ⇒ uncorrelated) If X and Y are independent, Q      then
  E(XY ) = E(X) E(Y ) and σXY = 0. More generally, E(X1 X2 · · · Xn ) =   E(Xi ) for
  independent Xi . [Freund Thms. 12–13]
  The converse is false. If X is uniform on {−1, 0, 1} and Y = X 2 , then Cov(X, Y ) =
  E X 3 − E X E X 2 = 0. Yet P (X = 0, Y = 0) = 13 ̸= 13 · 13 = P (X = 0)P (Y = 0), so X and Y
  are dependent. Covariance measures only linear association.


6.2.3 Moments of linear combinations

  Theorem 6.5. (One linear combination) If Y = ni=1 ai Xi , then
                                                 P

                X                       X                   X
        E(Y ) =   ai E(Xi ),  Var(Y ) =    a2i Var(Xi ) + 2   ai aj Cov(Xi , Xj ).
                    i                                i                   i<j

  If the Xi are independent, the covariance terms vanish. [Freund Thm. 14, Cor. 3]

                                                       P                    P
  Theorem 6.6. (Two linear combinations) If Y1 = ai Xi and Y2 =                bi Xi , then
                              X                  X
              Cov(Y1 , Y2 ) =   ai bi Var(Xi ) +  (ai bj + aj bi ) Cov(Xi , Xj ).

  For independent Xi , Cov(Y1 , Y2 ) =       i ai bi Var(Xi ). [Freund Thm. 15, Cor. 4]

6.2.4 Conditional expectation

   Definition 6.7. (Conditional expectation)
                               X                                              Z ∞
               E[u(X) | y] =        u(x)f (x | y)   or        E[u(X) | y] =         u(x)f (x | y) dx.
                                x                                             −∞

   In particular the conditional mean is µX|y = E(X | y), and the conditional variance is
                             2
                                 = E (X − µX|y )2 | y = E(X 2 | y) − µ2X|y .
                                                    
                            σX|y

   [Freund Def. 10, §8]

The recipe is always the same: (1) find the marginal of the conditioning variable; (2) form the
conditional PMF or PDF; (3) compute E(X | y) and E(X 2 | y) with respect to that conditional
distribution; (4) subtract.

  Theorem 6.8. (Iterated expectation and total variance (extension)) E(X | Y ) is a
  function of Y , hence a random variable, and
                                                                        
             E E(X | Y ) = E(X),       Var(X) = E Var(X | Y ) + Var E(X | Y ) .

  These are not proved in Freund’s main text, but they are the fastest route through two-stage
  (hierarchical) problems and a powerful check on conditional calculations. See [BT] §4.3, [CB]
  §4.4, [W] §3.5.


   Key formulas
   Covariance                  σXY = E(XY ) − µX µY ; ρ = σXY /(σX σY ) ∈ [−1, 1]
   Independence                ⇒ E(XY ) = E X E Y and σXY = 0 (not conversely)
                               Var( ai Xi ) = a2i σi2 + 2 i<j ai aj σij
                                   P          P          P
   Linear combination
                               Cov( ai Xi , bj Xj ) = i,j ai bj σij (with σii = σi2 )
                                    P       P          P
   Two combinations
   Conditional moments                           2
                               µX|y = E(X | y), σX|y = E(X 2 | y) − µ2X|y
   Tower / total variance      E X = E[E(X | Y )], Var X = E Var(X | Y ) + Var E(X | Y )

  Example 6.5 (Hierarchical model via the tower rule) A machine’s daily number of
  breakdowns Y is Poisson with mean X, where X itself varies from day to day, uniform on
  (0, 4). Find E Y and Var Y .
  Solution. E(Y | X) = X and Var(Y | X) = X. So E Y = E X = 2 and Var Y = E[Var(Y | X)] +
  Var[E(Y | X)] = E X + Var X = 2 + 16    10                                   4
                                     12 = 3 . The variability of the rate adds 3 beyond a pure
  Poisson with mean 2.                                                                      ■


6.4 Pitfalls and exam strategy

  Common pitfalls
                                                                                2 + 4σ 2 −
   ▶ Dropping the factor 2 on covariance terms, or the signs: Var(3X − 2Y ) = 9σX     Y
     12σXY .
   ▶ Constants in variances. Var(2X + 100) = 4 Var X; the 100 contributes nothing (Home-
     work 5, Problem 5).
   ▶ Using marginal moments for conditional ones. E(X | y) integrates against f (x | y),
     not g(x).
   ▶ E(XY ) over the wrong region. On a triangle, E(XY ) uses the triangle’s limits, exactly
     like a probability.
   ▶ “Cov = 0 so independent.” False in general (Theorem 6.4). It is true for the bivariate
     normal (Unit 8).


  Exam strategy
  For variance-of-a-combination questions, write the full expansion symbolically first, e.g. Var(W +
  V ) = Var W + Var V + 2 Cov(W, V ), and then reduce each piece with Var(aX + b) = a2 Var X
  and Cov(aX + b, cY + d) = ac Cov(X, Y ). It is hard to lose points this way. For conditional-
  moment questions, box the conditional density before integrating, and check that it integrates
  to 1.

7.2 Core concepts

7.2.1 The Bernoulli-trials family
A Bernoulli trial has two outcomes, success with probability θ and failure with probability 1 − θ.
Repeated independent trials generate three distributions, depending on what is counted.

   Definition 7.1. (Binomial       distribution) X = number of successes in n independent trials:
                 n x        n−x
   b(x; n, θ) = x θ (1−θ)        , x = 0, 1, . . . , n. Mean nθ, variance nθ(1−θ), MGF [1+θ(et −1)]n .
   Also b(x; n, θ) = b(n − x; n, 1 − θ), and the proportion X/n has mean θ and variance θ(1 − θ)/n.
   (n = 1 is the Bernoulli distribution.) [Freund Ch. 5, Defs. 2–3, Thms. 1–4]


   Definition 7.2. (Negative binomial and geometric               distributions) X = the trial on
   which the kth success occurs: b∗ (x; k, θ) = x−1
                                                        k        x−k , x = k, k + 1, . . . , with mean
                                                   k−1  θ  (1 − θ)
   k/θ and variance kθ 1θ − 1 . The case k = 1 is the geometric distribution g(x; θ) = θ(1 − θ)x−1 ,
                                

   x = 1, 2, . . . , with mean 1/θ and variance (1 − θ)/θ2 . [Freund Defs. 4–5, Thms. 5–6]

                                                                                n
Memorylessness: for the geometric, P
                                 x−1
                                      (X > m + n | X > m) = P (X > n) = (1 − θ) ; past failures
do not change the future. Why k−1 ? The xth trial must be the kth success, so the other k − 1
successes go among the first x − 1 trials.

7.2.3 The Poisson distribution

                                                                    λx e−λ
   Definition 7.4. (Poisson distribution) p(x; λ) =                        , x = 0, 1, 2, . . . , with mean λ,
                                  t
                                                                      x!
   variance λ, and MGF eλ(e −1) . [Freund Def. 7, Thms. 8–9]

▶ Poisson approximation to the binomial: if n is large and θ small, b(x; n, θ) ≈ p(x; nθ). It is
   good when n ≥ 20 and θ ≤ 0.05, and excellent when n ≥ 100 and nθ < 10. [Freund §7]
▶ Poisson process: if events occur at an average rate α per unit time (or area), the number in
   an interval of length t is Poisson with λ = αt. Rescale λ whenever the interval changes.

7.3 Worked examples

  Example 7.1 (Binomial: guessing) A student guesses on 8 four-option multiple-choice
  questions. Find P (at least 3 correct) and the mean and variance of the number correct.
  Solution. X ∼ b(x; 8, 14 ). By the complement, P (X ≥ 3) = 1 − (0.75)8 + 8(0.25)(0.75)7 +
                                                                   

  28(0.25)2 (0.75)6 = 1 − (0.1001 + 0.2670 + 0.3115) ≈ 0.321 . Mean 8 · 41 = 2, variance
                     

  8 · 41 · 34 = 1.5.                                                                      ■


  Example 7.2 (Hypergeometric versus binomial) A lot of 50 items contains 5 defectives.
  A sample of 4 is drawn without replacement. Find P (no defectives) exactly, and by the
  binomial approximation.
  Solution. Exactly: h(0; 4, 50, 5) = 45
                                         50
                                      4    4 = 148,995/230,300 ≈ 0.647. Binomial approxima-
  tion with θ = 0.1: 0.94 ≈ 0.656. The sample is 8% of the lot, above the 5% rule of thumb, so
  the approximation is only rough.                                                          ■


  Example 7.3 (Poisson process: rescaling λ) Typos occur at an average rate of 2 per page.
  Find the probability of (a) no typos on a page; (b) at most 3 typos on two pages.
  Solution. (a) λ = 2: e−2 ≈ 0.135. (b) For two pages, λ = 4: e−4 1 + 4 + 16   64
                                                                                  
                                                                           2 + 6 ≈ 0.433 .■



  Example 7.4 (Geometric and negative binomial) A player makes each free throw with
  probability 0.7, independently. Find (a) the probability that her first miss is on her 4th
  attempt; (b) the probability that her 3rd made shot comes on her 5th attempt.
  Solution. (a) The first  miss (“success” = miss, θ = 0.3) on trial 4: g(4; 0.3) = 0.73 (0.3) ≈ 0.103.
                       4
  (b) b∗ (5; 3, 0.7) = 2 (0.7)3 (0.3)2 = 6(0.343)(0.09) ≈ 0.185.

7.4 Pitfalls and exam strategy

   Common pitfalls
    ▶ With or without replacement? “Drawn from a lot” with no replacement and a small
      lot means hypergeometric, not binomial.
    ▶ Two conventions for the geometric. Freund counts trials (x = 1, 2, . . . , mean 1/θ).
      Some books count failures before the first success (x = 0, 1, . . . , mean (1 − θ)/θ). Read the
      definition in the problem.
    ▶ Not rescaling the Poisson rate when the interval changes (per minute vs. per hour).
    ▶ “At least one” is almost always 1 − P (X = 0).
    ▶ Approximation without a rule of thumb. Say why the approximation is justified, e.g.
      “n = 500 ≥ 100 and nθ = 2 < 10.”


   Exam strategy
   Start every problem by naming the random variable in words (“X = number of defectives
   in the sample of 5”) and the distribution with its parameters (“X ∼ h(x; 5, 20, 4)”). That
   sentence alone often earns a third of the credit, and it prevents most modelling errors.

7.6 Mock Exam 7

  Mock Exam 7: Special Discrete Distributions                                    Solutions → p. 109
  Time: 75 minutes · Total: 100 points        · Show all work; simplify exact answers; a scientific
  calculator is allowed.


 1. (10 pts) Ten percent of the items produced by a process are defective. A random sample of 12
    items is inspected.
     (a) Find the probability of exactly 2 defectives.
     (b) Find the probability of at most 1 defective.
     (c) Find the mean and variance of the number of defectives.

 2. (10 pts) A lot of 20 items contains 4 defectives. A sample of 5 is drawn without replacement.
     (a) Find the probability of exactly one defective.
     (b) Find the probability of at least one defective.
     (c) Find the mean and variance of the number of defectives.

 3. (10 pts) Calls arrive at a help desk according to a Poisson process at an average rate of 3 per
    minute.
     (a) Find the probability of exactly 5 calls in a 2-minute period.
     (b) Find the probability of at least 2 calls in a 1-minute period.

 4. (10 pts) Each of 500 independent components fails with probability 0.004.
     (a) Use the Poisson approximation to find the probability that at most one fails, and justify
         using it.
     (b) Write down, but do not evaluate, the exact binomial expression for the same probability.

 5. (10 pts) A trial succeeds with probability 0.2, independently. Let X be the trial on which the
    first success occurs.
     (a) Find P (X = 4) and P (X > 3).
     (b) Find P (X > 5 | X > 2) and explain the result.

 6. (10 pts) A trial succeeds with probability 0.4, independently. Find the probability that the
    3rd success occurs on the 7th trial, and the mean and variance of the number of trials needed
    to obtain 3 successes.

 7. (10 pts) A fair die is rolled 8 times. Find the probability of obtaining exactly two 1’s, exactly
    three outcomes from {2, 3}, and exactly three outcomes from {4, 5, 6}.

 8. (10 pts) Let X be binomial with parameters n and θ. Starting from MX (t) = [1 + θ(et − 1)]n ,
    derive E(X) and Var(X).

 9. (10 pts) A committee of 6 is chosen at random from 5 mathematicians, 4 statisticians, and 3
    computer scientists. Find the probability that it contains exactly 2 of each.

10. (10 pts) Name the distribution and its parameters for each random variable (no calculations).

    (a) The number of defectives in a sample of 10 drawn without replacement from a lot of 50
