8.3 Worked examples

                                                            R∞
  Example 8.1 (The gamma-function trick) Evaluate 0 x5 e−x/2 dx, and find E(X 3 ) when
  X is exponential with mean θ.
                                     R∞
  Solution. With α = 6, β = 2: 0 x5 e−x/2 dx = 26 Γ(6) = 64 · 120 = 7680. And E X 3 =
  R ∞ 3 1 −x/θ
   0 x θe      dx = 1θ θ4 Γ(4) = 6θ3 . No integration by parts needed.              ■


  Example 8.2 (Exponential waiting times) Buses arrive as a Poisson process at 4 per
  hour. Find the probability of waiting more than 20 minutes for a bus. If you have already
  waited 10 minutes, find the probability of waiting more than 20 additional minutes.
  Solution. The waiting time T (hours) is exponential with mean 14 , so P (T > 13 ) = e−4/3 ≈ 0.264.
  By memorylessness, P (T > 12 | T > 16 ) = P (T > 31 ) ≈ 0.264 as well.                          ■


  Example 8.3 (Normal probabilities and percentiles) Weights are normal with µ = 70 kg
  and σ = 8 kg. Find P (X > 80), P (60 < X < 80), and the 95th percentile.
  Solution. P (X > 80) = 1 − Φ(1.25) = 1 − 0.8944 = 0.1056, and P (60 < X < 80) =
  Φ(1.25) − Φ(−1.25) = 2(0.8944) − 1 = 0.7888. The 95th percentile is 70 + 1.645(8) ≈ 83.2 kg.




                                                    0.7888
                                                                      0.1056


                                       54      62     70         78    86
                                                       x


  Example 8.4 (Continuity correction) X is binomial with n = 50, θ = 0.4. Approximate
  P (X ≤ 15).
  Solution. nθ = 20 > 5 and n(1 − θ) = 30 > 5, so the approximation applies, with µ = 20 and



                                                      74
STA 431 · Study Guide                                                      Special Continuous Densities


      √
  σ = 12 ≈ 3.464. Then P (X ≤ 15) ≈ Φ 15.5−20
                                                   
                                             3.464   = Φ(−1.30) ≈ 0.097 . The exact value is
  0.0955; without the correction you would get Φ(−1.44) ≈ 0.075, much worse.              ■


  Example 8.5 (Bivariate normal prediction) Fathers’ and sons’ heights are bivariate
  normal with µX = 69, µY = 70, σX = σY = 3, and ρ = 0.5. For a father of height 75, find the
  distribution of his son’s height and P (Y > 75 | X = 75).
  Solution. µY |75 = 70 + 0.5 · 33 (75 − 69) = 73 and σY2 |75 = 9(1 − 0.25) = 6.75, so σ ≈ 2.598.
  Then P (Y > 75 | X = 75) = 1 − Φ 75−73
                                             
                                        2.598 = 1 − Φ(0.77) ≈ 0.221. The son is predicted to be
  tall, but closer to the mean than his father: regression to the mean.                        ■



8.4 Pitfalls and exam strategy

   Common pitfalls
    ▶ Scale versus rate. Freund’s exponential has mean θ (density 1 e−x/θ ); many other books
                                                                  θ
      use rate λ = 1/θ (density λe−λx ). Homework 5’s 3e−3x has mean 31 .
    ▶ Standardising with σ 2 instead of σ.
    ▶ Forgetting the continuity correction, or applying it in the wrong direction. Draw the
      bars around the integers.
    ▶ Reading the table backwards: Φ(z) is a left-tail area, so P (Z > z) = 1 − Φ(z).
    ▶ Bivariate normal conditional variance does not depend on x; only the mean shifts.



   Exam strategy
   Whenever an integral contains xk e−x/b on (0, ∞), recognise a gamma kernel and write the
                                                                   2 2
   answer down. Whenever an MGF has the form (1 − βt)−α or eµt+σ t /2 , name the distribution;
   uniqueness does the rest.

  Mock Exam 8: Special Continuous Densities                                     Solutions → p. 110
  Time: 75 minutes · Total: 100 points       · Show all work; simplify exact answers; a scientific
  calculator is allowed.


1. (8 pts) A bus arrives at a time uniformly distributed between 0 and 20 minutes after you reach
   the stop.
    (a) Find the probability that you wait more than 15 minutes, and the mean and variance of
        the wait.
    (b) Given that you have already waited 10 minutes, find the probability that you wait more
        than 15 minutes in total.

2. (10 pts) The lifetime of a component is exponential with mean 1000 hours.
    (a) Find the probability that it lasts more than 1500 hours.
    (b) Given that it has lasted 1000 hours, find the probability that it lasts more than 2500 hours.
    (c) Find the median lifetime.

3. (10 pts) Calls arrive as a Poisson process at 2 per hour. Let T be the time (in hours) until the
   third call.
    (a) Name the distribution of T and its parameters.
    (b) Find E T and Var T .
    (c) Find P (T ≤ 1).

4. (10 pts) A proportion X has density f (x) = 12x2 (1 − x) for 0 < x < 1.
    (a) Identify the distribution and find E X and Var X.
    (b) Find P (X > 21 ).

5. (12 pts) IQ scores are normal with mean 100 and standard deviation 15.
    (a) Find P (X > 130) and P (85 < X < 115).
    (b) Find the 90th percentile.

6. (12 pts) X is binomial with n = 100 and θ = 0.3.
    (a) Check the rule of thumb for the normal approximation.
    (b) Approximate P (X ≥ 35) with a continuity correction.

7. (10 pts)
    (a) Compute Γ(5) and Γ( 52 ).
                  R∞
    (b) Evaluate 0 x3 e−2x dx.
    (c) X has a chi-square distribution with 4 degrees of freedom. Find P (X > 6) by integrating
        its density.

8. (10 pts) (X, Y ) is bivariate normal with µX = 10, µY = 20, σX = 2, σY = 5, and ρ = 0.6.
    (a) Find the conditional distribution of Y given X = 12.
    (b) Find P (Y > 27 | X = 12).

 9. (10 pts)
     (a) Use MGFs to show that if X ∼ N(µ, σ 2 ), then aX + b ∼ N(aµ + b, a2 σ 2 ).

10. (8 pts) Customers arrive as a Poisson process at 4 per hour. Find the probability that no
    customer arrives in a 30-minute period, computing it both from the Poisson distribution of the
    count and from the exponential distribution of the waiting time.

Unit 9

Functions of Random Variables

    Final    Primary reading: Freund Ch. 7, §§1–5 (pp. 207–228)

   After this unit you should be able to
    ▶ find the distribution of Y = u(X) by the distribution-function (CDF) technique;
    ▶ use the transformation formula g(y) = f (w(y))|w ′ (y)|, including non-monotone maps split into
      branches;
    ▶ use the Jacobian method for two variables, introducing and integrating out an auxiliary variable;
    ▶ use MGFs to identify the distribution of sums of independent random variables;
    ▶ find the distributions of maxima and minima of independent variables, and apply the probability
      integral transform.

9.2 Core concepts

9.2.1 Distribution-function technique

   Theorem 9.1. (CDF technique) To find         the density of Y = u(X1 , . . . , Xn ): (1) write
   G(y) = P (Y ≤ y) = P u(X1 , . . . , Xn ) ≤ y ; (2) rewrite the event in terms of the X’s and
   compute it with their CDF or joint density; (3) differentiate, g(y) = G′ (y), and state the
   support. [Freund Ch. 7, §2]

This always works, even for non-monotone maps and for functions of several variables. For sums,
the event {X1 + X2 ≤ y} is a region below a line, as in Unit 4.

9.2.2 Transformation technique

   Theorem 9.2. (One variable) If y = u(x) is differentiable and strictly increasing or strictly
   decreasing on the support of X, with inverse x = w(y), then

                                    g(y) = f w(y) · |w′ (y)|
                                                   


   for y in the image of the support, and g(y) = 0 elsewhere. [Freund §3, Thm. 1]

If u is not monotone, split the support into pieces on which it is, and add √ the contributions
                                                                                       √         of all
                               ′                                       f (  y) +  f (−  y)
branches: g(y) = i f wi (y) |wi (y)|. For Y = X 2 , this gives g(y) =
                  P
                                                                                 √          for y > 0,
                                                                                2 y

                                                      81
STA 431 · Study Guide                                                          Functions of Random Variables

               √
provided both ± y lie in the support.

   Theorem 9.3. (Two variables (Jacobian)) If y1 = u1 (x1 , x2 ), y2 = u2 (x1 , x2 ) is one-to-
   one on the support with inverse x1 = w1 (y1 , y2 ), x2 = w2 (y1 , y2 ), then

                                                                ∂x1 /∂y1 ∂x1 /∂y2
                      g(y1 , y2 ) = f w1 , w2 · |J|,        J=                     .
                                                                 ∂x2 /∂y1 ∂x2 /∂y2

   For the density of a single function Y1 , choose a convenient auxiliary Y2 (often Y2 = X2 ), find
   the joint density, and integrate out y2 . [Freund §4, Thm. 2]

Mapping the support correctly is as important as the formula: transform each boundary of the old
support into the new coordinates.

   Theorem 9.4. (Sums of independent      Q random variables) If X1 , . . . , Xn are independent
   and Y = X1 + · · · + Xn , then MY (t) = i MXi (t). Identify Y by uniqueness of MGFs. [Freund
   §5, Thm. 3]

9.2.4 Extremes and the probability integral transform
For independent X1 , . . . , Xn with CDFs Fi :
                                     Y                                    Y               
               P (max Xi ≤ y) =        Fi (y),         P (min Xi > y) =         1 − Fi (y) .
                                       i                                   i
                                                                                    P
For independent exponentials with means θi , the minimum is exponential with rate i 1/θi . And
if X has a continuous, strictly increasing CDF F , then U = F (X) is uniform on (0, 1); conversely
F −1 (U ) has CDF F . [Freund Example 8, p. 216]

   Key formulas

  Monotone map      g(y) = f (w(y))|w′ (y)|
                                                                  √          √       √
  Non-monotone      sum over branches; Y = X 2 : g(y) = [f ( y) + f (− y)]/(2 y)
  Jacobian          g(y1 , y2 ) = f (w1 , w2 )|J|; integrate out the auxiliary variable
                                   Q
  MGF of a sum      MP Xi (t) = MXi (t) (independent)
  Linear map        MaX+b (t) = ebt MX (at)
                               Q                     Q
  Extremes          Fmax = Fi , 1 − Fmin = (1 − Fi )



9.3 Worked examples

  Example 9.1 (CDF technique) X has density 3x2 on (0, 1). Find the density of Y = 1/X.
  Solution. Y > 1. For y > 1, G(y) = P (1/X ≤ y) = P (X ≥ 1/y) = 1 − y −3 . Hence
  g(y) = 3y −4 for y > 1, and 0 otherwise. Check with Theorem 1: w(y) = 1/y, |w′ (y)| = y −2 , so
  g(y) = 3(1/y)2 · y −2 = 3y −4 .                                                              ■

9.4 Pitfalls and exam strategy

   Common pitfalls
     ▶ Missing |w ′ (y)|, or using u′ (x) instead of w ′ (y).
     ▶ Wrong support for Y . Map the endpoints: X ∈ (0, 1) and Y = − ln X give Y ∈ (0, ∞).
     ▶ Non-monotone maps with one branch only.                      For Y = X 2 on (−1, 2), values y < 1
       have two preimages but 1 < y < 4 only one.
     ▶ MGF technique without independence, or for products: MX+Y = MX MY needs
       independence, and nothing similar holds for XY .
     ▶ Sum of exponentials with different means is not gamma; the gamma result needs a
       common scale β.


   Exam strategy
   When unsure which technique to use, fall back on the CDF technique: it never fails, and it
   gives you something to differentiate. Use the transformation formula when u is monotone and
   you want speed. Use the MGF technique for any “sum of independent . . . ” question.

Sampling Distributions and the Central Limit
Theorem

    Final   Primary reading: Freund Ch. 8, §§1–7 (pp. 233–256)

   After this unit you should be able to
   ▶ compute the mean and standard error of X, with or without the finite-population correction;
   ▶ state the law of large numbers and the central limit theorem, and use the CLT for means and sums;
   ▶ explain why the CLT matters for sampling: X is approximately normal whatever the population;
   ▶ use the χ2 , t, and F sampling distributions for normal samples, and read their tables;
   ▶ derive the distributions of the minimum, maximum, and other order statistics.

10.2 Core concepts

   Definition 10.1. (Random sample; sample mean and variance) X1 , . . . , Xn is a random
   sample from a population if the Xi are independent  and identically distributed (iid) with
   that population’s distribution. Then X = n1                 1 P
                                                 Xi and S 2 = n−1  (Xi − X)2 . [Freund Ch. 8,
                                               P
   Defs. 1–3]


   Theorem 10.2. (Mean and variance of X; law of large numbers) If the population
                                                                                            √
   has mean µ and variance σ 2 , then E(X) = µ and Var(X) = σ 2 /n; the standard error is σ/ n.
                                           σ2
   By Chebyshev, P (|X − µ| < c) ≥ 1 − 2 → 1: the sample mean converges to µ. [Freund

   population with mean µ, variance σ 2 , and an MGF, then

                            X −µ

                                                       89
STA 431 · Study Guide                                   Sampling Distributions and the Central Limit Theorem


   Why the CLT matters
   It is why the normal distribution appears everywhere in statistics. Averages and totals of
   many independent effects are approximately normal whatever the shape of the individual
   effects, so one table (or one formula) handles means of exponential waiting times, uniform
   measurement errors, Bernoulli indicators (the normal approximation to the binomial is the
   CLT for a sum of Bernoullis), and so on. The proof (Freund pp. 236–237) shows that the
                          2
   MGF of Z tends to et /2 .

10.2.2 Order statistics

   Theorem 10.5. (Density of the rth order statistic) For a random sample of size n from
   a continuous population with density f and CDF F , the rth smallest value Yr has density
                                            n!
                          gr (y) =                     [F (y)]r−1 f (y) [1 − F (y)]n−r .
                                     (r − 1)! (n − r)!

   In particular the maximum has density nF (y)n−1 f (y) and the minimum has density n[1 −
   F (y)]n−1 f (y). [Freund Thm. 16]

Reading the formula: r − 1 observations fall below y, one falls at y, and n − r fall above y, and the
multinomial coefficient counts which is which.

10.4 Pitfalls and exam strategy

   Common pitfalls
                  √
   ▶ σ versus σ/ n. Questions about one observation use σ; questions about the mean use
       √                                  √
     σ/ n; questions about the total use σ n.
   ▶ The CLT is about X, not about X. It does not make the population normal.
   ▶ n versus n − 1 degrees of freedom: (n − 1)S 2 /σ 2 ∼ χ2
                                                           n−1 and T ∼ tn−1 .
                           2
   ▶ Upper-tail tables. χα,ν and tα,ν cut off area α to the right.
   ▶ Order statistics for discrete populations need the CDF method, P (max ≤ k) = F (k)n ,
     not the density formula.


   Exam strategy
   For any “approximately” question: (1) identify µ and σ 2 of a single observation; (2) scale
   to the mean or the total; (3) standardise; (4) apply a continuity correction if the quantity is
   integer-valued; (5) read the table. State “by the CLT” explicitly.

10.6 Mock Exam 10

  Mock Exam 10: Sampling Distributions and the CLT                                    Solutions →
  p. 113
  Time: 75 minutes · Total: 100 points       · Show all work; simplify exact answers; a scientific
  calculator is allowed.


1. (10 pts) A population has mean 50 and standard deviation 12. A random sample of 36 is
   taken.
    (a) Find the mean and standard error of X.
    (b) Approximate P (X > 53) and P (48 < X < 52).

2. (10 pts) Light bulbs have exponential lifetimes with mean 100 hours. Fifty bulbs are used one
   after another, each replacing the last as it fails. Approximate the probability that the total
   lifetime exceeds 5500 hours.

3. (10 pts) A population has standard deviation σ. How large must n be to guarantee P (|X −µ| <
   0.5σ) ≥ 0.95
    (a) using Chebyshev’s theorem;
    (b) using the CLT?
   Comment on the difference.

4. (8 pts) A sample of 25 is drawn without replacement from a population of 200 with standard
   deviation 10. Find the standard error of X, and compare it with the value ignoring the
   finite-population correction.

5. (10 pts) A sample of size 10 is taken from a normal population with σ 2 = 4.
    (a) Find c such that P (S 2 > c) = 0.05. (Use χ20.05,9 = 16.919.)
    (b) Find E(S 2 ) and Var(S 2 ).

6. (10 pts) A normal sample of size 16 has x̄ = 21.5 and s = 3. If µ = 20, compute the t statistic
   and bound P (T > t) using t0.05,15 = 1.753 and t0.025,15 = 2.131.

7. (8 pts) Independent samples of sizes 10 and 16 are drawn from normal populations with equal
   variances. Find P (S12 /S22 > 2.59), given F0.05,9,15 = 2.59, and explain which theorem you are
   using.

8. (14 pts) X1 , . . . , X5 is a random sample from the uniform distribution on (0, 1).
    (a) Find the density and the expected value of the maximum.
    (b) Find P (min > 0.2).
    (c) Find the density of the sample median and P (median < 21 ).

9. (10 pts)
    (a) Use Chebyshev’s theorem to prove the weak law of large numbers for X.
    (b) Outline how MGFs are used to prove the central limit theorem.



                                                94
STA 431 · Study Guide                          Sampling Distributions and the Central Limit Theorem


10. (10 pts) A fair coin is tossed 400 times. Use the normal approximation with a continuity
    correction to find the probability that the number of heads is between 190 and 210 inclusive.




                                               95
                 Part IV

Capstone Practice Exams
 Full-length exams in the format of Midterm I




                      96
How to Use the Capstone Exams
These two exams copy the format of Midterm I: numbered common problems with point values,
then an STA 431 only problem, 100 points in all. Take each one in a single sitting, with a time
limit, closed book, and a calculator, then grade it strictly against the solutions in Appendix A.
▶ Mock Midterm II covers Units 4–6 and follows the blueprint of Homeworks 4–5. Take it two
  or three days before the real exam, so there is time to repair weak spots.
▶ Mock Final is cumulative, weighted toward Units 7–10, with the earlier units represented too.
  Take it after finishing Mock Exams 7–10.




                                               97
STA 431 · Study Guide                                                               Mock Midterm II


Mock Midterm II

  Mock Midterm II                                                               Solutions → p. 114
  Time: 75 minutes · Total: 100 points       · Show all work; simplify exact answers; a scientific
  calculator is allowed.



Common Problems
1. (25 pts) Let the joint PMF of X and Y be f (x, y) = c(x + y) for x ∈ {1, 2, 3} and y ∈ {1, 2}.
    (a) (4 pts) Find the value of the constant c.
    (b) (6 pts) Find the marginal PMFs of X and Y .
    (c) (6 pts) Find the conditional PMF w(y | 1) and the conditional mean E(Y | X = 1).
    (d) (4 pts) Are X and Y independent? Justify your answer.
    (e) (5 pts) Find P (X > Y ).

2. (25 pts) Let the joint PDF of X and Y be f (x, y) = cx for 0 < y < x < 1, and 0 otherwise.
    (a) (5 pts) Find the value of c.
    (b) (6 pts) Find the marginal densities of X and Y .
    (c) (5 pts) Are X and Y independent? Justify your answer.
    (d) (5 pts) Find P (X + Y < 1).
    (e) (4 pts) Find the conditional density of Y given X = x, and P Y < 41 | X = 21 .
                                                                                    

3. (30 pts) The continuous random variable X has density f (x) = 4xe−2x for x > 0, and 0
   otherwise.
    (a) (10 pts) Derive the moment-generating function MX (t), stating the condition on t for
        which it exists.
    (b) (10 pts) Use the derivatives of MX (t) to find E(X) and Var(X).
    (c) (10 pts) Let Y = 3X − 1. Use the properties of MGFs to find MY (t), then use it to find
        E(Y ) and Var(Y ).

STA 431 Only Problems
4. (20 pts) The random variables X and Y have E(X) = 2, E(Y ) = −1, Var(X) = 4, Var(Y ) = 9,
   and Cov(X, Y ) = −3.
    (a) (5 pts) Calculate E(3X − 2Y + 5).
    (b) (5 pts) Calculate Var(3X − 2Y + 5).
    (c) (4 pts) Calculate Cov(X + Y, X − Y ).
    (d) (3 pts) Find the correlation coefficient of X and Y .
    (e) (3 pts) Find the product moment E(XY ).

STA 431 · Study Guide                                                              Mock Final Examination


Mock Final Examination

  Mock Final Examination (cumulative)                                                 Solutions → p. 115
  Time: 2 hours   · Total: 100 points   ·   Show all work; simplify exact answers; a scientific calculator
  is allowed.



Common Problems
1. (15 pts) (Units 1–2.) A box contains 6 red and 4 blue chips.
    (a) (5 pts) Three chips are drawn without replacement. Find the probability that exactly 2
        are red.
    (b) (5 pts) For the same draw, find the probability that exactly one chip is blue, given that at
        least one is blue.
    (c) (5 pts) A second box holds 3 red and 7 blue chips. A fair coin chooses one of the two boxes
        and a single chip is drawn from it. Find P (red), and the probability that the first box was
        chosen given that the chip is red.

2. (15 pts) (Units 3, 5.) Let f (x) = cx(2 − x) for 0 < x < 2, and 0 elsewhere.
    (a) (4 pts) Find c.
    (b) (4 pts) Derive the CDF F (x).
    (c) (3 pts) Calculate P (X > 1.5).
    (d) (4 pts) Find E(X) and Var(X).

3. (20 pts) (Units 4, 6.) Let f (x, y) = 2(x + y) for 0 < x < y < 1, and 0 elsewhere.
    (a) (6 pts) Find the marginal densities g(x) and h(y).
    (b) (6 pts) Find the conditional density f (x | y) and the conditional mean E(X | Y = y).
    (c) (8 pts) Find Cov(X, Y ).

4. (15 pts) (Units 5, 7–9.)
    (a) (5 pts) X1 ∼ Poisson(2) and X2 ∼ Poisson(3) are independent. Use MGFs to identify the
        distribution of X1 + X2 , and find P (X1 + X2 = 2).
    (b) (5 pts) X has MGF MX (t) = (0.5 + 0.5et )10 . Identify the distribution, give E X and Var X,
        and find P (X = 5).
    (c) (5 pts) X ∼ N(3, 4) and Y ∼ N(1, 5) are independent. Find the distribution of X − 2Y
        and P (X − 2Y > 1).

5. (15 pts) (Units 8–9.)
    (a) (8 pts) X is exponential with mean 1. Find the density of Y = e−X and identify it.
    (b) (7 pts) X has density 2x on (0, 1). Find the CDF and density of Y = − ln X.

STA 431 Only Problems
6. (20 pts) (Units 5, 8, 10.) The time to process a job has mean 4 minutes and standard deviation
   3 minutes. Times for different jobs are independent.


                                                   99
STA 431 · Study Guide                                                        Mock Final Examination


    (a) (6 pts) For 64 jobs, approximate the probability that the average time exceeds 4.5 minutes.
    (b) (6 pts) Approximate the probability that 64 jobs take 240 minutes or less in total.
    (c) (4 pts) Without using the CLT, give a lower bound for P (3.25 < X < 4.75) when n = 64.
    (d) (4 pts) A sample of 10 is taken from a normal population with σ 2 = 9. Find P (S 2 > 16.919),
        given χ20.05,9 = 16.919.

Appendix A

Solutions to the Mock Exams
Solutions are written the way a grader wants to see them: set-up first, then the computation, then
the answer, which is boxed. Exact answers are given, with decimals where they help. Read a
solution only after a full attempt.

2X − 5 ∼ N(2 · 3 − 5, 4 · 4) = N(1, 16), so P (2X − 5 > 3) = 1 − Φ 3−1  4    = 1 − Φ(0.5) = 0.3085 .

Problem 10. Count: N ∼ Poisson(4 × 12 = 2), so P (N = 0) = e−2 ≈ 0.1353 . Waiting time:
T ∼ exponential with mean 14 hour, so P (T > 21 ) = e−(1/2)/(1/4) = e−2 . These agree, as they must,
because “no arrival in the first 30 minutes” is the same event as “first arrival after 30 minutes”.

Problem 5. (a) Y ∈ (0, 1). For 0 < y < 1, G(y) = P (e−X ≤ y) = P (X ≥ − ln y) = eln y = y.
So Y is uniform on (0, 1), with density 1. This is the probability integral transform in disguise:
e−X = 1 − F (X). (b) Y = − ln X > 0, so G(y) = 0 for y ≤ 0. For y > 0, G(y) = P (− ln X ≤ y) =
P (X ≥ e−y ) = 1 − (e−y )2 = 1 − e−2y , so g(y) = 2e−2y for y > 0: exponential with mean 21 .

B.4 Standard normal distribution function
Entries are Φ(z) = P (Z ≤ z). For negative z use Φ(−z) = 1 − Φ(z). Useful quantiles: z0.10 = 1.282,
z0.05 = 1.645, z0.025 = 1.960, z0.01 = 2.326, z0.005 = 2.576.

            z      .00      .01      .02      .03      .04      .05      .06      .07      .08      .09
